S: scope='Parquet and ORC' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/columnar-storage] Predicate pushdown in Parquet and ORC enables Athena queries to fetch only the blocks it needs, improving query performance. === athena-compression-zlib-default-orc-gzip-default-parquet-text | athena | default | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: ZLIB is the default write compression format for ORC files. GZIP is the default write compression format for Parquet and text files in Hive tables in Athena engine versions 2 and 3 and Iceberg tables in Athena engine version 2.
Q: What is Athena's default write compression for ORC and for Parquet?
V: orc=ZLIB is the default write compression format || gzip=GZIP is the default write compression format
S: scope='Hive tables (engine versions 2 and 3) and Iceberg tables (engine version 2) for GZIP; ORC files for ZLIB' dom='default' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/compression-formats] GZIP – Compression algorithm based on Deflate. For Hive tables in Athena engine versions 2 and 3, and Iceberg tables in Athena engine version 2, GZIP is the default write compression format for files in the Parquet and text file storage formats. Files in the tar.gz format are not supported.
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/compression-formats] ZLIB – Based on Deflate, ZLIB is the default write compression format for files in the ORC data storage format.

Q: Which three grants does cross-account access to a KMS-encrypted bucket need?
V: bucket=bucket policy in Account A must grant access || role=access to both the bucket and the key
S: scope='cross-account Athena query of a bucket encrypted with a custom AWS KMS key' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/cross-account-permissions] The bucket policy in Account A must grant access to the role assumed by Account B.
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/cross-account-permissions] The AWS KMS key policy in Account A must grant access to the role assumed by the user in Account B.
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/cross-account-permissions] The AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role assumed by Account B must grant access to both the bucket and the key in Account A.
=== athena-cross-account-uploader-assume-role-avoids-object-acl | athena | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=1.3.K1
C: Objects uploaded by a third account (Account C) to Account A's bucket might need explicit object-level ACLs for the querying Account B. To avoid that, Account C should assume a role in Account A before it uploads.
Q: How do you avoid object ACL problems when a different account uploads objects that Athena queries?
V: acl=might require explicit object-level ACLs || role=Account C should assume a role
S: scope='cross-account access to Amazon S3 data from Athena' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/cross-account-permissions] Objects that are uploaded by an account (Account C) other than the bucket's owning account (Account A) might require explicit object-level ACLs that grant read access to the querying account (Account B). To avoid this requirement, Account C should assume a role in Account A before it places objects in Account A's bucket.

=== athena-ctas-external-location-must-be-empty | athena | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: The CTAS external_location must be empty. A CTAS query never overwrites data if the location already has data; delete the data under the prefix to reuse the location.
Q: Can a CTAS query write to an S3 location that already holds data?
V: empty=must specify a location that is empty || nooverwrite=never overwrites the data
S: scope='CTAS external_location property' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/ctas-considerations-limitations] The external_location property must specify a location that is empty. A CTAS query checks that the path location (prefix) in the bucket is empty and never overwrites the data if the location already has data in it. To use the same location again, delete the data in the key prefix location in the bucket.

E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/encryption] If you are working with a large number of encrypted objects and experience this issue, one option is to enable Amazon S3 bucket keys to reduce the number of calls to KMS.

E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/encryption] When you use Athena to read an encrypted table, Athena uses the encryption options specified for the table data, not the encryption option for the query results.
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/encryption] However, if you use Athena to insert data into a table that has encrypted data, Athena uses the encryption configuration that was specified for the query results to encrypt the inserted data.

This means a full table scan is avoided, meaning faster performance and lower query cost.

Q: Why can Athena time out on tables with many partitions, and what is the fix?
V: string=when you use type string || meta=Athena prunes partitions at the metastore level
S: scope='tables with many thousands of partitions' dom='' adj=False cond='partition type is not string'
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/other-notable-limitations] Timeouts on tables with many partitions – Athena may time out when querying a table that has many thousands of partitions. This can happen when the table has many partitions that are not of type string. When you use type string, Athena prunes partitions at the metastore level. However, when you use other data types, Athena prunes partitions on the server side.

=== athena-partition-projection-avoids-getpartitions-call | athena | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: Normally Athena calls GetPartitions on the AWS Glue Data Catalog before partition pruning, which can hurt performance for tables with many partitions. Partition projection lets Athena avoid that call.
Q: What call does partition projection avoid?
V: get=Athena makes a GetPartitions call || avoid=avoid calling GetPartitions
S: scope='tables with a large number of partitions' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/partition-projection] Normally, when processing queries, Athena makes a GetPartitions call to the AWS Glue Data Catalog before performing partition pruning. If a table has a large number of partitions, using GetPartitions can affect performance negatively. To avoid this, you can use partition projection. Partition projection allows Athena to avoid calling GetPartitions because the partition projection configuration gives Athena all of the necessary information to build the partitions itself.
=== athena-partition-projection-computes-from-table-properties | athena | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: With partition projection, Athena calculates partition values and locations from table properties configured on the table in AWS Glue, instead of a slower metadata lookup in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.
Q: How does partition projection find partitions?
V: calc=Athena calculates partition values and locations || nolookup=a more time-consuming metadata lookup
S: scope='Athena partition projection' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/partition-projection] In partition projection, Athena calculates partition values and locations using the table properties that you configure directly on your table in AWS Glue. The table properties allow Athena to 'project', or determine, the necessary partition information instead of having to do a more time-consuming metadata lookup in the AWS Glue Data Catalog.
=== athena-partition-projection-ignores-registered-partitions | athena | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7

=== athena-partition-projection-missing-partition-no-error | athena | behavior | hard=True | reference | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: If a projected partition does not exist in S3, Athena still projects it, throws no error and returns no data. If more than half of the projected partitions are empty, traditional partitions are recommended.
Q: What happens when a projected partition has no data in S3?
V: noerr=Athena does not throw an error || half=more than half of your projected partitions
S: scope='Athena partition projection' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/partition-projection] If a projected partition does not exist in Amazon S3, Athena will still project the partition. Athena does not throw an error, but no data is returned. However, if too many of your partitions are empty, performance can be slower compared to traditional AWS Glue partitions. If more than half of your projected partitions are empty, it is recommended that you use traditional partitions.

=== athena-s3-access-user-permissions-pass-through | athena | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=1.3.K1
C: When people use Athena, their own permissions pass through Athena to decide what Athena can access, so users must have permission to the S3 buckets in order to query them with Athena.

V: pass=their permissions pass through Athena || must=users must have permission to access Amazon S3 buckets
S: scope='Amazon Athena' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/s3-permissions] You can grant access to Amazon S3 locations using identity-based policies, bucket resource policies, access point policies, or any combination of the above. When actors interact with Athena, their permissions pass through Athena to determine what Athena can access. This means that users must have permission to access Amazon S3 buckets in order to query them with Athena.
=== athena-s3-requests-from-private-ip-no-sourceip-sourcevpc-conditions | athena | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=1.3.K1
C: Requests from Athena to S3 come from a private IPv4 address of Athena, so you cannot use aws:SourceIp to deny S3 access, and you cannot restrict S3 access with the aws:SourceVpc or aws:SourceVpce condition keys.
Q: Can an S3 policy use aws:SourceIp or aws:SourceVpce to control access by Athena?
V: ip=cannot use the aws:SourceIp condition to deny access || vpc=aws:SourceVpc or aws:SourceVpce condition keys
S: scope='S3 requests made by Athena' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/s3-permissions] Note that requests to Amazon S3 come from a private IPv4 address for Athena, not the source IP specified in aws:SourceIp. For this reason, you cannot use the aws:SourceIp condition to deny access to Amazon S3 actions in a given IAM policy. You also cannot restrict or allow access to Amazon S3 resources based on the aws:SourceVpc or aws:SourceVpce condition keys.

=== athena-tune-bucketing-high-cardinality-lookups | athena | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: Bucketing distributes records into separate files by the value of a column so that all records with the same value are in the same file. It is useful for a high-cardinality key when many queries look up specific values of that key.
Q: When is bucketing useful in Athena?
V: bucket=distributing records into separate files || card=a key with high cardinality
S: scope='Athena query performance tuning' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-data-optimization-techniques] Bucketing is a technique for distributing records into separate files based on the value of one of the columns. This ensures that all records with the same value will be in the same file. Bucketing is useful when you have a key with high cardinality and many of your queries look up specific values of the key.
=== athena-tune-bucketing-less-valuable-multiple-values | athena | behavior | hard=False | reference | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: Bucketing is less valuable when queries frequently search for multiple values in the bucketed column, because more files may have to be read.
Q: When should you avoid bucketing in Athena?
V: less=Bucketing is less valuable || multi=frequently search for multiple values in the column
S: scope='Athena query performance tuning' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-data-optimization-techniques] Bucketing is less valuable when queries frequently search for multiple values in the column that the data is bucketed by. The more values queried, the higher the likelihood that all or most files will have to be read.

=== athena-tune-combine-small-files-before-they-are-written | athena | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7,3.5.K4

E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-data-optimization-techniques] Put files in a location on Amazon S3 and use a tool like Glue ETL to combine them into larger files. Then, move the larger files into the location that the table points to.
=== athena-tune-compressed-data-cheaper-bytes-before-decompression | athena | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7,3.5.K1

=== athena-tune-compressed-text-files-not-processed-in-parallel | athena | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: Most compression formats require reading a file from the beginning, so compressed text files generally cannot be processed in parallel, while big uncompressed files are often split between workers.
Q: Why can compressed CSV or JSON files limit Athena parallelism?
V: begin=reader to read files from the beginning || noparallel=cannot, in general, be processed in parallel
S: scope='compressed text files such as JSON and CSV' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-data-optimization-techniques] Most compression formats require the reader to read files from the beginning. This means that compressed text files cannot, in general, be processed in parallel. Big uncompressed files are often split between workers to achieve higher parallelism during query processing, but this is not possible with most compression formats.

=== athena-tune-include-only-required-columns | athena | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: Leave out columns you do not strictly need from an Athena query: less data to process runs faster, and with a columnar format fewer columns also means less data read from S3.
Q: Why avoid SELECT * in Athena?
V: less=The less data a query has to process || s3=reduces the amount of data that is read
S: scope='Athena query performance tuning' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-query-optimization-techniques] If you don't strictly need a column, don't include it in your query. The less data a query has to process, the faster it will run. This reduces both the amount of memory required and the amount of data that has to be sent between nodes. If you are using a columnar file format, reducing the number columns also reduces the amount of data that is read from Amazon S3.
=== athena-tune-join-smaller-table-on-the-right | athena | behavior | hard=True | reference | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K1
C: In an Athena join with an equality condition, the table to the right of JOIN is the build side and must be the smaller table, with the larger table on the left.
Q: Which table goes on the right of a JOIN in Athena?
V: right=the right table, the build side || left=table to the left of the JOIN keyword
S: scope='joins with equality-based join conditions' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-query-optimization-techniques] When writing joins with equality-based join conditions, assume that the table to the left of the JOIN keyword is the probe side and the table to the right is the build side. Make sure that the right table, the build side, is the smaller of the tables.
=== athena-tune-many-small-files-poor-performance | athena | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: Datasets made of many small files give poor overall query performance because Athena lists all partition locations and each file adds handling and request overhead. Loading a single bigger file from S3 is faster than many smaller files.
Q: Why are many small files bad for Athena?
V: poor=small files result in poor overall query performance || bigger=loading a single bigger file from Amazon S3 is faster
S: scope='Athena query performance tuning' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-data-optimization-techniques] Datasets that consist of many small files result in poor overall query performance. When Athena plans a query, it lists all partition locations, which takes time. Handling and requesting each file also has a computational overhead. Therefore, loading a single bigger file from Amazon S3 is faster than loading the same records from many smaller files.
=== athena-tune-materialize-common-subqueries-with-ctas | athena | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7

V: mat=materialize the common subquery as a new table || stale=must keep the new table up to date
S: scope='Athena query performance tuning' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-query-optimization-techniques] If many of your queries include the same joins and aggregations, you can materialize the common subquery as a new table and then run queries against that table. You can create the new table with Create a table from query results (CTAS), or a dedicated ETL tool like Glue ETL.
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-query-optimization-techniques] The disadvantage is that you must keep the new table up to date.

=== athena-tune-partition-keys-not-too-many-not-too-few | athena | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7

Conversely, having too few partition keys, or no partitioning at all, leads to queries that scan more data than necessary.

=== athena-tune-remove-partition-entries-when-data-removed | athena | behavior | hard=False | reference | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: If you remove data from a partition in S3, for example with an S3 lifecycle rule, also remove the partition entry from the AWS Glue Data Catalog, because Athena lists every matched partition on S3 during query planning and many empty partitions add overhead.
Q: Why remove partition entries after deleting data from S3?
V: remove=partition entry from the AWS Glue Data Catalog || empty=If you have many empty partitions
S: scope='Athena query performance tuning' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-data-optimization-techniques] If you remove data from a partition on Amazon S3 (for example, by using Amazon S3 lifecycle), you should also remove the partition entry from the AWS Glue Data Catalog. During query planning, any partition matched by the query is listed on Amazon S3. If you have many empty partitions, the overhead of listing these partitions can be detrimental.
=== athena-tune-reuse-query-results-max-age | athena | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=1.3.K1,3.5.K1
C: You can tell Athena to reuse previously calculated results and specify the maximum age of results to reuse. If the same query ran within that time frame, Athena returns those results instead of running the query again.
Q: How does Athena query result reuse work?
V: reuse=tell Athena to reuse previously calculated results || age=maximum age of the results to be reused
S: scope='Athena query performance tuning' dom='' adj=False cond='the same query was run within the maximum age'
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-query-optimization-techniques] When you run a query, you can tell Athena to reuse previously calculated results. You specify the maximum age of the results to be reused. If the same query was previously run within that time frame, Athena returns those results instead of running the query again.
=== athena-tune-unload-for-large-result-sets | athena | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: Athena stores a regular query result as a single uncompressed CSV file on S3, which cannot be parallelized. For large results, UNLOAD is usually faster and can write compressed output and other formats such as JSON and Parquet.
Q: Why use UNLOAD for large Athena result sets?
V: csv=result as a single uncompressed CSV file || unload=UNLOAD writes results directly from the worker nodes
S: scope='Athena query performance tuning' dom='' adj=False cond='results expected to be large'
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/performance-tuning-query-optimization-techniques] When a query finishes executing, Athena stores the result as a single uncompressed CSV file on Amazon S3. This takes longer than UNLOAD, not only because the result is uncompressed, but also because the operation cannot be parallelized. In contrast, UNLOAD writes results directly from the worker nodes and makes full use of the parallelism of the compute cluster.
=== athena-unload-formats-vs-select-csv-only | athena | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: The Athena SELECT command outputs only CSV. UNLOAD rewrites a SELECT query's output to other formats: Apache Parquet, ORC, Apache Avro and JSON.
Q: How can you get Athena query results in Parquet instead of CSV without creating a table?
V: unload=Apache Parquet, ORC, Apache Avro, and JSON || csv=CSV is the only output format supported
S: scope='the Athena UNLOAD statement' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/unload] Supported formats for UNLOAD include Apache Parquet, ORC, Apache Avro, and JSON. CSV is the only output format supported by the Athena SELECT command, but you can use the UNLOAD command, which supports a variety of output formats, to enclose your SELECT query and rewrite its output to one of the formats that UNLOAD supports.
=== athena-unload-max-100-partitions | athena | limit | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7

=== athena-unload-target-must-be-empty-no-overwrite | athena | restriction | hard=True | reference | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S7
C: Except when writing to partitions, the UNLOAD destination must be an S3 location with no data. UNLOAD verifies the location is empty and does not overwrite existing data.
Q: Can UNLOAD overwrite existing data in an S3 location?
V: empty=location in Amazon S3 that has no data || nooverwrite=UNLOAD does not overwrite existing data
S: scope='UNLOAD TO destination, not partitioned writes' dom='' adj=False cond='except when writing to partitions'
E: [docs/athena/latest/ug/unload] Except when writing to partitions, the TO destination must specify a location in Amazon S3 that has no data. Before the UNLOAD query writes to the location specified, it verifies that the bucket location is empty. Because UNLOAD does not write data to the specified location if the location already has data in it, UNLOAD does not overwrite existing data.

=== emr-ebs-volumes-deleted-at-termination-no-snapshot-restore | emr | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S5
C: EBS volumes attached to EMR clusters are deleted on cluster and instance termination, and you cannot snapshot an EBS volume and restore it within Amazon EMR.
Q: Do EBS volumes on an EMR cluster keep their data after termination?
V: v0=volumes are deleted upon cluster and instance termination || v1=can't snapshot an Amazon EBS volume || ephemeral=are ephemeral
S: scope='Amazon EMR on EC2 clusters' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/emr/latest/ManagementGuide/emr-plan-storage] Amazon EBS volumes attached to Amazon EMR clusters are ephemeral: the volumes are deleted upon cluster and instance termination (for example, when shrinking instance groups), so you shouldn't expect data to persist.
E: [docs/emr/latest/ManagementGuide/emr-plan-storage] You can't snapshot an Amazon EBS volume and then restore it within Amazon EMR.

=== emr-hdfs-ephemeral-reclaimed-at-cluster-end | emr | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-numbers+changes | bul=3.5.S5
C: In Amazon EMR, HDFS is used by the master and core nodes; it is fast, but it is ephemeral storage which is reclaimed when the cluster ends, so it is best used for caching the results produced by intermediate job-flow steps.
Q: EMR file systems: is HDFS persistent after the cluster ends?
V: ephemeral=ephemeral storage which is reclaimed
S: scope='HDFS on EMR cluster nodes' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/emr/latest/ManagementGuide/emr-plan-file-systems] H{| File system | Prefix | Description |} | HDFS | hdfs:// (or no prefix) | HDFS is a distributed, scalable, and portable file system for Hadoop. An advantage of HDFS is data awareness between the Hadoop cluster nodes managing the clusters and the Hadoop cluster nodes managing the individual steps. For more information, see [Hadoop documentation](http://hadoop.apache.org/docs/stable). <br />HDFS is used by the master and core nodes. One advantage is that it's fast; a disadvantage is that it's ephemeral storage which is reclaimed when the cluster ends. It's best used for caching the results produced by intermediate job-flow steps.  |

=== glue-classifier-determines-schema-built-in-and-custom | glue | capability | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K4
C: A Glue classifier determines the schema of your data. Glue provides classifiers for common file types such as CSV, JSON, AVRO and XML and for common relational databases over JDBC, and you can write your own with a grok pattern or an XML row tag.
Q: What does a Glue classifier do and can you write your own?
V: schema=Determines the schema of your data || custom=write your own classifier
S: scope='AWS Glue' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/components-key-concepts] Determines the schema of your data. AWS Glue provides classifiers for common file types, such as CSV, JSON, AVRO, XML, and others. It also provides classifiers for common relational database management systems using a JDBC connection. You can write your own classifier by using a grok pattern or by specifying a row tag in an XML document.

=== glue-crawler-athena-zero-results-different-schemas-same-prefix | glue | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K1,3.5.K4
C: In Athena each table is an S3 prefix with all its objects. If a crawler creates multiple tables from one prefix because objects have different schemas, Athena queries might return zero results.
Q: Why might Athena return zero results for a table that a Glue crawler created?
V: zero=queries in Athena that return zero results || path=each table corresponds to an Amazon S3 prefix
S: scope='AWS Glue crawlers' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/add-crawler] In Amazon Athena, each table corresponds to an Amazon S3 prefix with all the objects in it. If objects have different schemas, Athena does not recognize different objects within the same prefix as separate tables. This can happen if a crawler creates multiple tables from the same Amazon S3 prefix. This might lead to queries in Athena that return zero results.

=== glue-crawler-first-matching-custom-classifier-then-built-in | glue | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K4
C: A crawler runs your custom classifiers in the order you specify, and the first one to recognize the data structure creates the schema. If none matches, built-in classifiers, for example one that recognizes JSON, try to recognize the schema.
Q: In what order does a Glue crawler try classifiers?
V: first=first custom classifier to successfully recognize || builtin=built-in classifiers try to recognize your data's schema
S: scope='AWS Glue crawlers' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/add-crawler] The first custom classifier to successfully recognize the structure of your data is used to create a schema. Custom classifiers lower in the list are skipped.
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/add-crawler] If no custom classifier matches your data's schema, built-in classifiers try to recognize your data's schema. An example of a built-in classifier is one that recognizes JSON.

For Amazon S3, you can optionally specify a connection of type Network.

A crawler can crawl multiple data stores in a single run. Upon completion, the crawler creates or updates one or more tables in your Data Catalog.
=== glue-crawler-rerun-finds-new-or-changed-files-and-partitions | glue | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K4,3.5.S1

=== glue-crawler-s3-event-mode-first-crawl-full-listing | glue | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K4

E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/crawler-s3-event-notifications] The first crawl lists all Amazon S3 objects from the target. After the first successful crawl, you can choose to recrawl manually or on a set schedule. The crawler will list only the objects from those events instead of listing all objects.
=== glue-crawler-s3-event-mode-limitations | glue | restriction | hard=True | reference | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K4
C: An S3 event crawler supports only a single target, does not support SQS on a private VPC, and does not support Amazon S3 sampling.
Q: What are the limitations of Glue S3 event crawlers?
V: single=Only a single target is supported || vpc=SQS on private VPC is not supported
S: scope='S3 event mode crawlers' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/crawler-s3-event-notifications] Only a single target is supported by the crawler, whether for Amazon S3 or Data Catalog targets.
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/crawler-s3-event-notifications] SQS on private VPC is not supported.
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/crawler-s3-event-notifications] Amazon S3 sampling is not supported.
=== glue-crawler-s3-event-mode-lists-only-changed-folders | glue | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K4
C: A crawler can use Amazon S3 events to find changes instead of listing the whole S3 or Data Catalog target, which improves recrawl time by listing only the subfolders that triggered events.

V: events=use Amazon S3 events to find any changes || fast=improves the recrawl time
S: scope='AWS Glue crawlers' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/crawler-s3-event-notifications] Instead of listing the objects from an Amazon S3 or Data Catalog target, you can configure the crawler to use Amazon S3 events to find any changes. This feature improves the recrawl time by using Amazon S3 events to identify the changes between two crawls by listing all the files from the subfolder which triggered the event instead of listing the full Amazon S3 or Data Catalog target.

There will be no cost if there are no events in the queue. Amazon S3 events can be configured to go directly to the SQS queue or in cases where multiple consumers need the same event, a combination of SNS and SQS.

That will result in missing or skipping files in the input path B as AWS Glue would assume that those files had been processed in previous runs.

=== glue-job-bookmark-tracks-processed-data-prevents-reprocessing | glue | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K4,3.5.S7
C: AWS Glue tracks data already processed in a previous ETL job run by persisting state information called a job bookmark. Job bookmarks prevent reprocessing of old data, so you can process only new data when rerunning on a scheduled interval.
Q: What does a Glue job bookmark do?
V: state=persisting state information from the job run || new=new data when rerunning on a scheduled interval
S: scope='AWS Glue job bookmarks' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/monitor-continuations] AWS Glue tracks data that has already been processed during a previous run of an ETL job by persisting state information from the job run. This persisted state information is called a job bookmark. Job bookmarks help AWS Glue maintain state information and prevent the reprocessing of old data. With job bookmarks, you can process new data when rerunning on a scheduled interval.

=== glue-partition-index-getpartitions-subset-instead-of-all | glue | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S1,3.5.S7
C: Without a partition index, AWS Glue loads all partitions of the table and then filters them for a GetPartitions request, which slows down as partitions increase. With an index, GetPartitions fetches only a subset of the partitions.
Q: What does a Glue partition index change for GetPartitions?
V: noindex=loads all the partitions of the table || index=fetch a subset of the partitions
S: scope='Glue Data Catalog tables' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/partition-indexes] If no partition indexes are present on the table, AWS Glue loads all the partitions of the table, and then filters the loaded partitions using the query expression provided by the user in the GetPartitions request. The query takes more time to run as the number of partitions increase on a table with no indexes. With an index, the GetPartitions query will try to fetch a subset of the partitions instead of loading all the partitions in the table.

E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/partition-indexes] Each partition index item will be charged according to the current AWS Glue pricing policy for data catalog storage.
=== glue-partition-index-only-and-operator-others-ignored | glue | restriction | hard=True | reference | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.S1
C: Glue partition indexes currently support only the AND logical operator. Sub-expressions with LIKE, IN, OR and NOT are ignored for index filtering and are applied afterwards to the fetched partitions.
Q: Which logical operators work with Glue partition indexes?
V: and=currently only supports the AND logical operator || ignored=operators "LIKE", "IN", "OR", and "NOT" are ignored
S: scope='Glue partition indexes' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/partition-indexes] The indexing solution currently only supports the AND logical operator. Sub-expressions with the operators "LIKE", "IN", "OR", and "NOT" are ignored in the expression for filtering using an index. Filtering for the ignored sub-expression is done on the partitions fetched after applying index filtering.

=== glue-r-worker-m-dpu-32-gb | glue | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-numbers+changes | bul=3.5.K4
C: AWS Glue R type workers use Memory-Optimized DPUs (M-DPUs): a standard DPU provides 16 GB of memory, while an M-DPU in R type workers provides 32GB of memory for memory-intensive Spark applications.
Q: AWS Glue: how much memory does an M-DPU of an R worker have compared with a standard DPU?
V: m-dpu=32GB of memory || standard=16 GB of memory
S: scope='R type (memory-optimized) workers' dom='fixed' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/worker-types] **Memory-Optimized DPUs (M-DPUs)**: R type workers use M-DPUs, which provide double the memory allocation for a given size compared to standard DPUs. This means that while a standard DPU provides 16 GB of memory, an M-DPU in R type workers provides 32GB of memory optimized for memory-intensive Spark applications.

=== glue-streaming-default-100-second-window-checkpoints-not-bookmarks | glue | default | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K4
C: By default, AWS Glue streaming jobs process and write out data in 100-second windows, which you can modify. They use checkpoints rather than job bookmarks to track the data that has been read.
Q: What is the default processing window of a Glue streaming job, and how does it track progress?
V: window=100-second windows || ckpt=use checkpoints rather than job bookmarks
S: scope='AWS Glue streaming ETL jobs' dom='default' adj=True cond='window size can be modified'
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/add-job-streaming] By default, AWS Glue processes and writes out data in 100-second windows. This allows data to be processed efficiently and permits aggregations to be performed on data arriving later than expected. You can modify this window size to increase timeliness or aggregation accuracy. AWS Glue streaming jobs use checkpoints rather than job bookmarks to track the data that has been read.

=== glue-streaming-kafka-needs-connection-kinesis-does-not | glue | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K4

E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/add-job-streaming] When creating a streaming ETL job for Amazon Kinesis Data Streams, you don't have to create an AWS Glue connection. However, if there is a connection attached to the AWS Glue streaming ETL job that has Kinesis Data Streams as a source, then a virtual private cloud (VPC) endpoint to Kinesis is required.

=== glue-trigger-three-types-scheduled-conditional-on-demand | glue | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=3.5.K4
C: Glue has three trigger types. Scheduled is a time-based trigger based on cron. Conditional fires when previous jobs or crawlers satisfy a list of conditions. On-demand fires when you activate it and stays in the CREATED state.

V: sched=A time-based trigger based on cron || cond=jobs or crawlers satisfy a list of conditions
S: scope='AWS Glue triggers' dom='' adj=False cond=''

E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/about-triggers] A trigger that fires when a previous job or crawler or multiple jobs or crawlers satisfy a list of conditions.
E: [docs/glue/latest/dg/about-triggers] A trigger that fires when you activate it. On-demand triggers never enter the ACTIVATED or DEACTIVATED state. They always remain in the CREATED state.

You can configure a network address translation (NAT) instance inside your VPC, or you can use the Amazon VPC NAT gateway.

=== lake-formation-credential-vending-engine-filters-columns-rows | lake-formation | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=1.3.K1,3.5.S1
C: If the user is authorized, Lake Formation provides temporary access to the integrated engine, which fetches the data from S3 and performs the column, row or cell filtering. This process is called credential vending.

V: temp=provides temporary access to the integrated analytical engine || cv=This process is called credential vending
S: scope='AWS Lake Formation' dom='' adj=False cond='the user is authorized and the data is registered with Lake Formation'
E: [docs/lake-formation/latest/dg/how-it-works] Get data – If the user is authorized to access the table, Lake Formation provides temporary access to the integrated analytical engine. Using the temporary access, the analytical engine fetches the data from Amazon S3, and performs necessary filtering such as column, row, or cell filtering. When the engine finishes running the job, it returns the results back to the user. This process is called credential vending.

=== lake-formation-data-filter-belongs-to-table-partiql-where-row-expression | lake-formation | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=1.3.K1
C: Each data filter belongs to a specific Data Catalog table. Its row filter expression specifies the rows to include in query results, and with some restrictions it uses the syntax of a WHERE clause in the PartiQL language.

S: scope='Lake Formation data filters' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/lake-formation/latest/dg/data-filtering] Each data filter belongs to a specific table in your Data Catalog.
E: [docs/lake-formation/latest/dg/data-filtering] Row filter expression – an expression that specifies the rows to include in query results. With some restrictions, the expression has the syntax of a WHERE clause in the PartiQL language.
=== lake-formation-data-filter-populating-decides-column-row-or-cell | lake-formation | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=1.3.K1

V: row=you are establishing row-level security (row filtering) only || cell=you are establishing cell-level security (cell filtering)
S: scope='Lake Formation data filters' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/lake-formation/latest/dg/data-filtering] When you specify the "all columns" wildcard and provide a row filter expression, you are establishing row-level security (row filtering) only.
E: [docs/lake-formation/latest/dg/data-filtering] When you include or exclude specific columns and nested columns, and specify "all rows" using the all-rows wildcard, you are establishing column-level security (column filtering) only.
E: [docs/lake-formation/latest/dg/data-filtering] When you include or exclude specific columns and also provide a row filter expression, you are establishing cell-level security (cell filtering).

This is to prevent users from creating tables in arbitrary locations within the data lake and provides control over where those users can read and write data.

E: [docs/lake-formation/latest/dg/access-control-underlying-data] Amazon Athena requires the user to have the lakeformation:GetDataAccess permission. Other integrated services require their underlying execution role to have the lakeformation:GetDataAccess permission.

=== lake-formation-group-and-principal-row-permissions-are-union | lake-formation | behavior | hard=False | reference | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=1.3.K1
C: If a principal and its group are both granted permissions on subsets of rows, the principal's effective row permissions are the union of both. In a cross-account case the effective predicate is the intersection of the account's predicate and the one granted to the principal.
Q: How are overlapping row filters combined for a principal and its group?
V: union=the union of the principal's permissions || inter=the intersection of the account's predicate
S: scope='row-level and cell-level filtering' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/lake-formation/latest/dg/data-filtering-notes] If a principal is a member of a group and both the principal and the group are granted permissions on a subset of rows, the principal's effective row permissions are the union of the principal's permissions and the group's permissions.
E: [docs/lake-formation/latest/dg/data-filtering-notes] When permissions on a subset of rows are granted to an external AWS account and the data lake administrator of the external account grants those permissions to a principal in that account, the principal's effective filter predicate is the intersection of the account's predicate and any predicate that was directly granted to the principal.

=== lake-formation-vs-iam-who-controls-what | lake-formation | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=1.3.K1
C: Lake Formation permissions control access to Data Catalog resources, S3 locations and the underlying data, while IAM permissions control access to the Lake Formation and AWS Glue APIs and resources. A Lake Formation CREATE_TABLE permission still fails without the IAM permission on glue:CreateTable.
Q: What do IAM permissions control versus Lake Formation permissions?
V: lf=Lake Formation permissions control access to Data Catalog resources, Amazon S3 locations || iam=IAM permissions control access
S: scope='AWS Lake Formation' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/lake-formation/latest/dg/lf-permissions-overview] Lake Formation permissions control access to Data Catalog resources, Amazon S3 locations, and the underlying data at those locations. IAM permissions control access to the Lake Formation and AWS Glue APIs and resources. So although you might have the Lake Formation permission to create a metadata table in the Data Catalog (CREATE_TABLE), your operation fails if you don't have the IAM permission on the glue:CreateTable API.

=== opensearch-multiaz-standby-three-zones-failover-under-a-minute | opensearch | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-numbers+changes | bul=3.5.K1
C: With Multi-AZ with Standby, OpenSearch Service creates a domain across three Availability Zones with a complete copy of data in each zone, reserves nodes in one zone as standby that don't serve search requests, and automatically activates the standby nodes in less than a minute when it detects a failure in the underlying infrastructure.
Q: OpenSearch Service Multi-AZ with Standby: how many AZs and how fast is the failover?
V: zones=three Availability Zones || failover=in less than a minute || v0=which means that they don't serve search requests || v1=automatically activates the standby nodes
S: scope='Multi-AZ with Standby domains' dom='fixed' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/opensearch-service/latest/developerguide/managedomains-multiaz] When you use Multi-AZ with Standby, OpenSearch Service creates a domain across three Availability Zones, with each zone containing a complete copy of data and with the data equally distributed in each of the zones. Your domain reserves nodes in one of these zones as standby, which means that they don't serve search requests. When OpenSearch Service detects a failure in the underlying infrastructure, it automatically activates the standby nodes in less than a minute.

=== quick-pricing-agent-hours-definition | quick | pricing-dimension | hard=True | reference | frequently-changing | by=mine-35-numbers+changes | bul=3.5.S4

V: unit=metered per second
S: scope='Amazon Quick AI features across desktop, web, mobile and extensions' dom='' adj=False cond=''

Agent hours are consumed regardless of the interface you use: the desktop app, web, mobile app, or extensions. Usage is metered per second.

=== quick-rls-enterprise-restrict-dataset-readers | quick | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-35-behavior+decisions | bul=1.3.K1,3.5.S4
C: In the Enterprise edition of Amazon Quick you can restrict access to a dataset with row-level security (RLS); dataset owners still see all the data while readers see only data allowed by the permission dataset rules.
Q: How do you restrict which rows Quick users can see?
V: v0=configuring row-level security (RLS) || v1=they can only see the data restricted
S: scope='Enterprise Edition' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/quick/latest/userguide/row-level-security] In the Enterprise edition of Amazon Quick, you can restrict access to a dataset by configuring row-level security (RLS) on it. You can do this before or after you have shared the dataset. When you share a dataset with RLS with dataset owners, they can still see all the data. When you share it with readers, however, they can only see the data restricted by the permission dataset rules.

E: [docs/quick/latest/userguide/spice] In the formula below, decimals and dates are calculated as 8 bytes per cell with 4 extra bytes for auxillary. Strings are calculated based on the text's length in UTF-8 encoding plus 24 bytes for auxillary.
